At work, there have been chances where I could’ve helped lead a meeting or taken on more of the supervisory parts of things, and I just… I don’t volunteer. I’ll tell myself I’m too busy or that someone else would do it better, but really it’s because I picture everyone sitting there looking at my face, especially this area under my jaw and neck, instead of listening to me.\n\nAnd it affects the regular parts of my job too. Sometimes I get stuck before work trying to make myself look presentable, adjusting my hair or collar, and then I feel rushed and scattered once I get there. I’ve fallen behind on paperwork because I’m already behind from all that, or because I’m distracted thinking about how I look when people come up to talk to me.\n\nWith relationships… I still see people, but I’ve stopped going to some things I used to enjoy. I’m in a book club, and there’s a walking group too, and I’ve skipped them more than once because getting ready just feels like this whole exhausting process. By the time I’ve checked and fixed everything, I’m upset or embarrassed, and I think, “I can’t go now.” So I just stay home, even though part of me knows I probably would’ve felt better if I went.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about this uneven spot on your face when you're not intentionally trying to think about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time, honestly. That’s really why I came in, because it’s not just when I’m standing in front of the mirror on purpose. It’s like my mind goes right back to that spot around my jaw and neck even when I’m trying to think about something else.\n\nI’ll be at work, talking with families or nurses, and part of me is trying to listen and do my job, but another part of me is thinking, “They’re looking at it. They can see that sagging there. They notice one side doesn’t sit right.” Even if nobody says anything, I just feel like it’s the first thing people see when they look at me.\n\nAnd at church too… I’ll be sitting there trying to pay attention, and then I’ll get this thought about whether the light is hitting my neck in a bad way, or whether someone behind me can see it from the side. People tell me I look normal, or they say they don’t know what I’m talking about, but it doesn’t really settle in. I still feel like it stands out so much. It’s just kind of always there in the back of my mind.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when you think about this uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: It gets really upsetting. I mean, I know maybe that sounds vague, but it’s not just a little annoyance where I notice it and move on. When I think about that uneven, sagging area around my jaw and neck, I feel this drop in my stomach, like I’ve suddenly been caught looking awful or something.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I feel like I can’t stop seeing it. Even if I’m not trying to look for it, my mind goes right there, and then once I’m thinking about it, it’s hard to pull myself away from it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, people have said it looks normal, they don’t see what you see,” but it doesn’t feel normal to me. It feels like it’s the first thing families at work notice, or nurses, or people at church when they look at me.\n\nSo the distress is… I’d say pretty strong. Not every single second is the same, but when it hits, I feel embarrassed and tense and kind of ashamed, like I want to hide that part of my face or make sure I’m turned the right way. It can take over my whole mood. Even if someone is being perfectly nice to me, I’m sitting there wondering if they’re just being polite and actually noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about other parts of your body besides your face and neck?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I can have normal complaints like anyone, but the thing I can’t get away from is my face and neck, especially that sagging, uneven spot around my jaw. That’s the part I came in about, because I just can’t stop seeing it.\n\nEven when people tell me, “You look fine,” or “I don’t notice anything,” it doesn’t land for me. I still feel like it’s the first thing families at work see, or the nurses I’m around, or people at church when they look at me. So no, it’s not really my whole body. It’s very focused on that area, and it feels like it stands out so much more than anyone else seems to think it does.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and neck actually look uneven, or are you afraid that they might look uneven to others?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it actually looks uneven. That’s the hard part. It doesn’t feel like just a “what if” to me. When I look at it, especially around my jaw and down into my neck, I see this sagging, uneven spot, like one side just doesn’t sit the way it should.\n\nAnd then when I’m around other people, that turns into this worry that they’re seeing exactly what I’m seeing. Like at work, when I’m with families or talking with nurses, I feel like their eyes go right there, even if they’re not saying anything. Or at church, I’ll be trying to just be present, but I’m thinking, “They can notice it from this angle.”\n\nPeople have told me it looks normal, or that they don’t see what I mean, but I don’t really believe it deep down. I came in because I can’t stop seeing it, and it feels like it’s the first thing people notice when they look at my face.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern that your face and neck actually look uneven, or that others might think you look unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly that I think it actually looks uneven, that specific area. I mean, I do worry people will think I look bad because of it, but it’s not like I’m walking around thinking every part of me is unattractive in general. It’s more like my mind locks onto that sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck, and then I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nAnd once I see it, I assume other people see it too. Like families at work, or nurses I’m around, or people at church… I feel like when they look at my face, that’s the first thing they notice. Even when people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really feel reassured. I keep thinking maybe they’re just being kind or they don’t want to say it. So yes, it’s about that unevenness more than just a general fear that I’m unattractive.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and neck ever caused you to avoid social interactions or important events because you felt too self-conscious about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t always call it “avoiding” in my head, because I’ll make it sound more practical, like, “Oh, I’m too tired,” or “I have paperwork to catch up on,” or “It’s just not a good night.” But if I’m being honest, a lot of it comes back to not wanting people looking at my face and neck.\n\nAt work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or take on more of the supervisory-type pieces, even when I probably could do them, because the thought of standing up there or having everyone looking at me just makes me feel… exposed. I start thinking they’re going to notice that uneven sagging around my jaw, and then I get so distracted by that thought that I don’t feel like I can focus on what I’m supposed to be saying.\n\nAnd even getting ready in the morning can take so long sometimes because I’m trying to make myself look presentable, or at least less noticeable, and then I end up behind on paperwork because I’ve spent so much time checking and fixing and trying to feel okay enough to go out.\n\nSocially, too, I’ve stopped going to some of my book club things and walking club activities. Not all of them, but enough that people have noticed. It’s just that getting ready feels like this whole ordeal, and then once I’m there I’m thinking about my neck instead of enjoying myself. So sometimes it feels easier to just not go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your perception of your face and neck is accurate, or could it be that you're seeing something that isn't really there?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows you’re asking whether maybe I’m seeing it wrong, and I wish I could say, “Yes, maybe it’s not really there.” But when I look in the mirror, it feels accurate to me. It feels like I can see this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and down into my neck, and once I notice it, I can’t unsee it.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in, because I can’t stop seeing it. Even if other people tell me, “No, it looks normal,” or “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” it doesn’t really settle in for me. I’ll think, well, maybe they’re just being polite, or they didn’t look closely, or they don’t want to embarrass me.\n\nAnd then when I’m around families at work, or nurses, or people at church, I feel like it’s right there, like it’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. So… I guess I can understand that maybe I’m seeing it more strongly than other people do, but it doesn’t feel imaginary to me. It feels very real when I see it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance mostly about this specific uneven spot on your face, or is it more about feeling unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one specific area, the uneven spot around my jaw and neck. I’m not saying I never worry about how I look overall, because of course once I get upset about that spot, then I start feeling ugly or embarrassed in a more general way. But the thing that starts it, the thing I came in about, is that sagging, uneven-looking part.\n\nIt’s like my eyes go straight to it every time I see myself. And then I start thinking other people are seeing the same thing. At work, with the families or the nurses, or at church, I’ll feel like when they look at me, that’s what they notice first. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” or “I don’t see anything,” I don’t really believe it. I keep thinking they’re just being nice or they don’t want to hurt my feelings.\n\nSo no, it’s not really just feeling unattractive in general. It’s very focused on that area of my face and neck.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides the uneven spot on your face and neck, that you worry other people might notice or comment on?\n\nPatient: Not really anything separate, no. I mean, I can pick at other things if I’m already upset, like my face in general or whether I look older or tired, but that’s not really the main thing I’m afraid people are noticing.\n\nIt keeps coming back to that same sagging, uneven-looking spot around my jaw and into my neck. That’s what I came in about, because I just can’t stop seeing it. Even when people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really believe them. I feel like when families at work look at me, or the nurses, or people at church, that’s the first thing their eyes go to. And then I start wondering if they’re thinking about it or if they’ll say something, even if they don’t.\n\nSo… no, it’s not like I have a whole list of features I’m worried about. It’s mostly that one area, and then everything else kind of spirals from there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the unevenness you see in your face and neck isn't actually there?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess there’s a tiny part of me that can say maybe it isn’t as obvious as it feels to me. I know people keep telling me it looks normal, and I hear that. But when I’m actually looking at myself, especially around my jaw and down into my neck, I can’t make myself believe it’s not there.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because I can’t stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot. It’s like my eyes go right to it, and then I start thinking, how could anyone else not see it too? When I’m with families at work, or around the nurses, or even people at church, I feel like it’s the first thing they notice when they look at me.\n\nSo… is there a possibility? I suppose maybe, in some logical way. But it doesn’t feel that way to me. It feels real, and when people say they don’t see it, I just think they’re being kind or trying not to embarrass me.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance something that you think would be resolved if this one uneven spot on your face and neck were fixed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, in my mind, if that one spot around my jaw and neck were fixed, I feel like I could finally stop thinking about it all the time. That’s what I keep coming back to. I came in because I can’t stop seeing that sagging, uneven-looking area, and it feels like everything else starts from there.\n\nLike, if I’m getting ready for work or church, I’m not standing there thinking about ten different things. Even when people say it looks normal, I don’t really feel reassured.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do think if that one area were fixed, I’d feel a lot better. Maybe I’d stop checking it so much or stop feeling like everyone is looking right there. I know that probably sounds like I’m putting a lot on one little area, but it doesn’t feel little to me. It feels like the whole problem.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I think… if it were really completely fixed, I want to say no, I wouldn’t be so self-conscious. Because it really does all come back to that one sagging, uneven-looking spot around my jaw and into my neck. That’s the thing I came in about. I can’t stop seeing it.

Like at work, when I’m talking to families, I feel like they’re looking right at that area. Or the nurses, when they’re standing close to me, I’ll wonder if they noticed it. Even at church, I feel like people’s eyes go there first when they look at my face.

So if that part were somehow completely fixed and looked even and normal to me, I think I’d be much less self-conscious. Maybe I’d still have normal worries, like anybody my age might, but not this constant feeling that there’s something wrong that everyone is noticing. Even when people tell me it looks normal now, I just don’t feel like I can believe them, because to me it stands out so much.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came because I just can’t get past this thing with my face, especially around my jaw and my neck. It’s like there’s this sagging, uneven spot there, and I see it every time I look in the mirror. I know other people say it looks normal, or they act like they don’t know what I’m talking about, but to me it’s just… right there.\n\nAnd I feel like when families come in where I work, or nurses, or even people at church, that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like they’re looking right at that part of my jaw before they even hear what I’m saying. I’ve tried to tell myself maybe I’m making too much of it, but I can’t stop seeing it. It’s been really hard to just go about my day without thinking everybody can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to fix or check this uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably want to admit. Before I leave for work, especially, I’ll think I’m ready, like I have my bag and my keys and everything, and then I’ll end up going back into the bathroom just to check one more time. I’ll look at my neck and my jaw from the front, then kind of turn to the side, and then I’ll fix my collar or pull my hair forward a certain way so it covers that area better.\n\nAnd then I’ll leave the bathroom, but sometimes I don’t even make it to the door before I turn around and go back again. I’ll check it in my phone camera too, because I think maybe the mirror is making it look different, but then the phone makes me look at it even more. If I have a meeting or I know I’m going to be around people, it gets worse.\n\nSometimes I ask my daughter, like, “Does this look strange?” or “Can you see this right here?” and I know she gets tired of me asking. She’ll say it looks fine, but I don’t really feel reassured for very long. It’s embarrassing, but yes, I keep checking and adjusting, and I’ve been late more than once because I couldn’t stop going back to look.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other habits or things you feel like you need to do over and over that aren't related to your face or appearance?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I’m trying to think if there’s anything else like that, like checking the stove or washing my hands a certain number of times or things people talk about, but nothing really comes to mind.\n\nI can be particular about how I get ready in the morning, but honestly that mostly circles back to how I look before I go out. I don’t feel like I have other rituals that I have to do over and over, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s mostly this… this checking and fixing with my face and neck.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your face and neck affected your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I like to admit. At work, there have been chances where I could’ve helped lead a meeting or taken on more of the supervisory parts of things, and I just… I don’t volunteer. I’ll tell myself I’m too busy or that someone else would do it better, but really it’s because I picture everyone sitting there looking at my face, especially this area under my jaw and neck, instead of listening to me.\n\nAnd it affects the regular parts of my job too. Sometimes I get stuck before work trying to make myself look presentable, adjusting my hair or collar, and then I feel rushed and scattered once I get there. I’ve fallen behind on paperwork because I’m already behind from all that, or because I’m distracted thinking about how I look when people come up to talk to me.\n\nWith relationships… I still see people, but I’ve stopped going to some things I used to enjoy. I’m in a book club, and there’s a walking group too, and I’ve skipped them more than once because getting ready just feels like this whole exhausting process. By the time I’ve checked and fixed everything, I’m upset or embarrassed, and I think, “I can’t go now.” So I just stay home, even though part of me knows I probably would’ve felt better if I went.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about this uneven spot on your face when you're not intentionally trying to think about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time, honestly. That’s really why I came in, because it’s not just when I’m standing in front of the mirror on purpose. It’s like my mind goes right back to that spot around my jaw and neck even when I’m trying to think about something else.\n\nI’ll be at work, talking with families or nurses, and part of me is trying to listen and do my job, but another part of me is thinking, “They’re looking at it. They can see that sagging there. They notice one side doesn’t sit right.” Even if nobody says anything, I just feel like it’s the first thing people see when they look at me.\n\nAnd at church too… I’ll be sitting there trying to pay attention, and then I’ll get this thought about whether the light is hitting my neck in a bad way, or whether someone behind me can see it from the side. People tell me I look normal, or they say they don’t know what I’m talking about, but it doesn’t really settle in. I still feel like it stands out so much. It’s just kind of always there in the back of my mind.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when you think about this uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: It gets really upsetting. I mean, I know maybe that sounds vague, but it’s not just a little annoyance where I notice it and move on. When I think about that uneven, sagging area around my jaw and neck, I feel this drop in my stomach, like I’ve suddenly been caught looking awful or something.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I feel like I can’t stop seeing it. Even if I’m not trying to look for it, my mind goes right there, and then once I’m thinking about it, it’s hard to pull myself away from it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, people have said it looks normal, they don’t see what you see,” but it doesn’t feel normal to me. It feels like it’s the first thing families at work notice, or nurses, or people at church when they look at me.\n\nSo the distress is… I’d say pretty strong. Not every single second is the same, but when it hits, I feel embarrassed and tense and kind of ashamed, like I want to hide that part of my face or make sure I’m turned the right way. It can take over my whole mood. Even if someone is being perfectly nice to me, I’m sitting there wondering if they’re just being polite and actually noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about other parts of your body besides your face and neck?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I can have normal complaints like anyone, but the thing I can’t get away from is my face and neck, especially that sagging, uneven spot around my jaw. That’s the part I came in about, because I just can’t stop seeing it.\n\nEven when people tell me, “You look fine,” or “I don’t notice anything,” it doesn’t land for me. I still feel like it’s the first thing families at work see, or the nurses I’m around, or people at church when they look at me. So no, it’s not really my whole body. It’s very focused on that area, and it feels like it stands out so much more than anyone else seems to think it does.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and neck actually look uneven, or are you afraid that they might look uneven to others?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it actually looks uneven. That’s the hard part. It doesn’t feel like just a “what if” to me. When I look at it, especially around my jaw and down into my neck, I see this sagging, uneven spot, like one side just doesn’t sit the way it should.\n\nAnd then when I’m around other people, that turns into this worry that they’re seeing exactly what I’m seeing. Like at work, when I’m with families or talking with nurses, I feel like their eyes go right there, even if they’re not saying anything. Or at church, I’ll be trying to just be present, but I’m thinking, “They can notice it from this angle.”\n\nPeople have told me it looks normal, or that they don’t see what I mean, but I don’t really believe it deep down. I came in because I can’t stop seeing it, and it feels like it’s the first thing people notice when they look at my face.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern that your face and neck actually look uneven, or that others might think you look unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly that I think it actually looks uneven, that specific area. I mean, I do worry people will think I look bad because of it, but it’s not like I’m walking around thinking every part of me is unattractive in general. It’s more like my mind locks onto that sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck, and then I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nAnd once I see it, I assume other people see it too. Like families at work, or nurses I’m around, or people at church… I feel like when they look at my face, that’s the first thing they notice. Even when people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really feel reassured. I keep thinking maybe they’re just being kind or they don’t want to say it. So yes, it’s about that unevenness more than just a general fear that I’m unattractive.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and neck ever caused you to avoid social interactions or important events because you felt too self-conscious about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t always call it “avoiding” in my head, because I’ll make it sound more practical, like, “Oh, I’m too tired,” or “I have paperwork to catch up on,” or “It’s just not a good night.” But if I’m being honest, a lot of it comes back to not wanting people looking at my face and neck.\n\nAt work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or take on more of the supervisory-type pieces, even when I probably could do them, because the thought of standing up there or having everyone looking at me just makes me feel… exposed. I start thinking they’re going to notice that uneven sagging around my jaw, and then I get so distracted by that thought that I don’t feel like I can focus on what I’m supposed to be saying.\n\nAnd even getting ready in the morning can take so long sometimes because I’m trying to make myself look presentable, or at least less noticeable, and then I end up behind on paperwork because I’ve spent so much time checking and fixing and trying to feel okay enough to go out.\n\nSocially, too, I’ve stopped going to some of my book club things and walking club activities. Not all of them, but enough that people have noticed. It’s just that getting ready feels like this whole ordeal, and then once I’m there I’m thinking about my neck instead of enjoying myself. So sometimes it feels easier to just not go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your perception of your face and neck is accurate, or could it be that you're seeing something that isn't really there?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows you’re asking whether maybe I’m seeing it wrong, and I wish I could say, “Yes, maybe it’s not really there.” But when I look in the mirror, it feels accurate to me. It feels like I can see this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and down into my neck, and once I notice it, I can’t unsee it.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in, because I can’t stop seeing it. Even if other people tell me, “No, it looks normal,” or “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” it doesn’t really settle in for me. I’ll think, well, maybe they’re just being polite, or they didn’t look closely, or they don’t want to embarrass me.\n\nAnd then when I’m around families at work, or nurses, or people at church, I feel like it’s right there, like it’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. So… I guess I can understand that maybe I’m seeing it more strongly than other people do, but it doesn’t feel imaginary to me. It feels very real when I see it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance mostly about this specific uneven spot on your face, or is it more about feeling unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one specific area, the uneven spot around my jaw and neck. I’m not saying I never worry about how I look overall, because of course once I get upset about that spot, then I start feeling ugly or embarrassed in a more general way. But the thing that starts it, the thing I came in about, is that sagging, uneven-looking part.\n\nIt’s like my eyes go straight to it every time I see myself. And then I start thinking other people are seeing the same thing. At work, with the families or the nurses, or at church, I’ll feel like when they look at me, that’s what they notice first. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” or “I don’t see anything,” I don’t really believe it. I keep thinking they’re just being nice or they don’t want to hurt my feelings.\n\nSo no, it’s not really just feeling unattractive in general. It’s very focused on that area of my face and neck.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides the uneven spot on your face and neck, that you worry other people might notice or comment on?\n\nPatient: Not really anything separate, no. I mean, I can pick at other things if I’m already upset, like my face in general or whether I look older or tired, but that’s not really the main thing I’m afraid people are noticing.\n\nIt keeps coming back to that same sagging, uneven-looking spot around my jaw and into my neck. That’s what I came in about, because I just can’t stop seeing it. Even when people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really believe them. I feel like when families at work look at me, or the nurses, or people at church, that’s the first thing their eyes go to. And then I start wondering if they’re thinking about it or if they’ll say something, even if they don’t.\n\nSo… no, it’s not like I have a whole list of features I’m worried about. It’s mostly that one area, and then everything else kind of spirals from there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the unevenness you see in your face and neck isn't actually there?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess there’s a tiny part of me that can say maybe it isn’t as obvious as it feels to me. I know people keep telling me it looks normal, and I hear that. But when I’m actually looking at myself, especially around my jaw and down into my neck, I can’t make myself believe it’s not there.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because I can’t stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot. It’s like my eyes go right to it, and then I start thinking, how could anyone else not see it too? When I’m with families at work, or around the nurses, or even people at church, I feel like it’s the first thing they notice when they look at me.\n\nSo… is there a possibility? I suppose maybe, in some logical way. But it doesn’t feel that way to me. It feels real, and when people say they don’t see it, I just think they’re being kind or trying not to embarrass me.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance something that you think would be resolved if this one uneven spot on your face and neck were fixed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, in my mind, if that one spot around my jaw and neck were fixed, I feel like I could finally stop thinking about it all the time. That’s what I keep coming back to. I came in because I can’t stop seeing that sagging, uneven-looking area, and it feels like everything else starts from there.\n\nLike, if I’m getting ready for work or church, I’m not standing there thinking about ten different things. My eyes go right to that part of my face and neck, and then I start worrying that families at work are going to notice it, or the nurses are going to see it when they’re talking to me, or people at church will look at me and that’s the first thing they’ll notice. Even when people say it looks normal, I don’t really feel reassured.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do think if that one area were fixed, I’d feel a lot better. Maybe I’d stop checking it so much or stop feeling like everyone is looking right there. I know that probably sounds like I’m putting a lot on one little area, but it doesn’t feel little to me. It feels like the whole problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be self-conscious about your appearance if the uneven spot on your face and neck were completely fixed?\n\nPatient: I think… if it were really completely fixed, I want to say no, I wouldn’t be so self-conscious. Because it really does all come back to that one sagging, uneven-looking spot around my jaw and into my neck. That’s the thing I came in about. I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nI mean, when I look in the mirror, my eyes go right there. And then once I see it, I start thinking about everybody else seeing it too. Even at church, I feel like people’s eyes go there first when they look at my face.\n\nSo if that part were somehow completely fixed and looked even and normal to me, I think I’d be much less self-conscious. Maybe I’d still have normal worries, like anybody my age might, but not this constant feeling that there’s something wrong that everyone is noticing. Even when people tell me it looks normal now, I just don’t feel like I can believe them, because to me it stands out so much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly… yes, I think I would still worry. I wish I could say that if everybody around me told me it was fine, I’d just accept it and move on, but that hasn’t really been how it works for me.

People do tell me it looks normal, or they’ll say they don’t notice anything, and I know they probably mean well. But when I look at myself, especially that sagging, uneven place around my jaw and down into my neck, it just jumps out at me. It’s like I can’t unsee it once I’ve noticed it.

And then when I’m at work with families, or talking to the nurses, or even sitting with people at church, I still feel like that’s the first thing they’re seeing when they look at me. Even if they say they don’t, part of me thinks maybe they’re just being polite, or maybe they noticed but don’t want to make me feel bad.

So yes… even if everyone said I looked fine, I think I’d still be stuck on it, because I can’t stop seeing it myself.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came because I just can’t get past this thing with my face, especially around my jaw and my neck. It’s like there’s this sagging, uneven spot there, and I see it every time I look in the mirror. I know other people say it looks normal, or they act like they don’t know what I’m talking about, but to me it’s just… right there.\n\nAnd I feel like when families come in where I work, or nurses, or even people at church, that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like they’re looking right at that part of my jaw before they even hear what I’m saying. I’ve tried to tell myself maybe I’m making too much of it, but I can’t stop seeing it. It’s been really hard to just go about my day without thinking everybody can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to fix or check this uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably want to admit. Before I leave for work, especially, I’ll think I’m ready, like I have my bag and my keys and everything, and then I’ll end up going back into the bathroom just to check one more time. I’ll look at my neck and my jaw from the front, then kind of turn to the side, and then I’ll fix my collar or pull my hair forward a certain way so it covers that area better.\n\nAnd then I’ll leave the bathroom, but sometimes I don’t even make it to the door before I turn around and go back again. I’ll check it in my phone camera too, because I think maybe the mirror is making it look different, but then the phone makes me look at it even more. If I have a meeting or I know I’m going to be around people, it gets worse.\n\nSometimes I ask my daughter, like, “Does this look strange?” or “Can you see this right here?” and I know she gets tired of me asking. She’ll say it looks fine, but I don’t really feel reassured for very long. It’s embarrassing, but yes, I keep checking and adjusting, and I’ve been late more than once because I couldn’t stop going back to look.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other habits or things you feel like you need to do over and over that aren't related to your face or appearance?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I’m trying to think if there’s anything else like that, like checking the stove or washing my hands a certain number of times or things people talk about, but nothing really comes to mind.\n\nI can be particular about how I get ready in the morning, but honestly that mostly circles back to how I look before I go out. I don’t feel like I have other rituals that I have to do over and over, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s mostly this… this checking and fixing with my face and neck.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your face and neck affected your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I like to admit. At work, there have been chances where I could’ve helped lead a meeting or taken on more of the supervisory parts of things, and I just… I don’t volunteer. I’ll tell myself I’m too busy or that someone else would do it better, but really it’s because I picture everyone sitting there looking at my face, especially this area under my jaw and neck, instead of listening to me.\n\nAnd it affects the regular parts of my job too. Sometimes I get stuck before work trying to make myself look presentable, adjusting my hair or collar, and then I feel rushed and scattered once I get there. I’ve fallen behind on paperwork because I’m already behind from all that, or because I’m distracted thinking about how I look when people come up to talk to me.\n\nWith relationships… I still see people, but I’ve stopped going to some things I used to enjoy. I’m in a book club, and there’s a walking group too, and I’ve skipped them more than once because getting ready just feels like this whole exhausting process. By the time I’ve checked and fixed everything, I’m upset or embarrassed, and I think, “I can’t go now.” So I just stay home, even though part of me knows I probably would’ve felt better if I went.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about this uneven spot on your face when you're not intentionally trying to think about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time, honestly. That’s really why I came in, because it’s not just when I’m standing in front of the mirror on purpose. It’s like my mind goes right back to that spot around my jaw and neck even when I’m trying to think about something else.\n\nI’ll be at work, talking with families or nurses, and part of me is trying to listen and do my job, but another part of me is thinking, “They’re looking at it. They can see that sagging there. They notice one side doesn’t sit right.” Even if nobody says anything, I just feel like it’s the first thing people see when they look at me.\n\nAnd at church too… I’ll be sitting there trying to pay attention, and then I’ll get this thought about whether the light is hitting my neck in a bad way, or whether someone behind me can see it from the side. People tell me I look normal, or they say they don’t know what I’m talking about, but it doesn’t really settle in. I still feel like it stands out so much. It’s just kind of always there in the back of my mind.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when you think about this uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: It gets really upsetting. I mean, I know maybe that sounds vague, but it’s not just a little annoyance where I notice it and move on. When I think about that uneven, sagging area around my jaw and neck, I feel this drop in my stomach, like I’ve suddenly been caught looking awful or something.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I feel like I can’t stop seeing it. Even if I’m not trying to look for it, my mind goes right there, and then once I’m thinking about it, it’s hard to pull myself away from it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, people have said it looks normal, they don’t see what you see,” but it doesn’t feel normal to me. It feels like it’s the first thing families at work notice, or nurses, or people at church when they look at me.\n\nSo the distress is… I’d say pretty strong. Not every single second is the same, but when it hits, I feel embarrassed and tense and kind of ashamed, like I want to hide that part of my face or make sure I’m turned the right way. It can take over my whole mood. Even if someone is being perfectly nice to me, I’m sitting there wondering if they’re just being polite and actually noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about other parts of your body besides your face and neck?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I can have normal complaints like anyone, but the thing I can’t get away from is my face and neck, especially that sagging, uneven spot around my jaw. That’s the part I came in about, because I just can’t stop seeing it.\n\nEven when people tell me, “You look fine,” or “I don’t notice anything,” it doesn’t land for me. I still feel like it’s the first thing families at work see, or the nurses I’m around, or people at church when they look at me. So no, it’s not really my whole body. It’s very focused on that area, and it feels like it stands out so much more than anyone else seems to think it does.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and neck actually look uneven, or are you afraid that they might look uneven to others?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it actually looks uneven. That’s the hard part. It doesn’t feel like just a “what if” to me. When I look at it, especially around my jaw and down into my neck, I see this sagging, uneven spot, like one side just doesn’t sit the way it should.\n\nAnd then when I’m around other people, that turns into this worry that they’re seeing exactly what I’m seeing. Like at work, when I’m with families or talking with nurses, I feel like their eyes go right there, even if they’re not saying anything. Or at church, I’ll be trying to just be present, but I’m thinking, “They can notice it from this angle.”\n\nPeople have told me it looks normal, or that they don’t see what I mean, but I don’t really believe it deep down. I came in because I can’t stop seeing it, and it feels like it’s the first thing people notice when they look at my face.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern that your face and neck actually look uneven, or that others might think you look unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly that I think it actually looks uneven, that specific area. I mean, I do worry people will think I look bad because of it, but it’s not like I’m walking around thinking every part of me is unattractive in general. It’s more like my mind locks onto that sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck, and then I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nAnd once I see it, I assume other people see it too. Like families at work, or nurses I’m around, or people at church… I feel like when they look at my face, that’s the first thing they notice. Even when people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really feel reassured. I keep thinking maybe they’re just being kind or they don’t want to say it. So yes, it’s about that unevenness more than just a general fear that I’m unattractive.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and neck ever caused you to avoid social interactions or important events because you felt too self-conscious about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t always call it “avoiding” in my head, because I’ll make it sound more practical, like, “Oh, I’m too tired,” or “I have paperwork to catch up on,” or “It’s just not a good night.” But if I’m being honest, a lot of it comes back to not wanting people looking at my face and neck.\n\nAt work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or take on more of the supervisory-type pieces, even when I probably could do them, because the thought of standing up there or having everyone looking at me just makes me feel… exposed. I start thinking they’re going to notice that uneven sagging around my jaw, and then I get so distracted by that thought that I don’t feel like I can focus on what I’m supposed to be saying.\n\nAnd even getting ready in the morning can take so long sometimes because I’m trying to make myself look presentable, or at least less noticeable, and then I end up behind on paperwork because I’ve spent so much time checking and fixing and trying to feel okay enough to go out.\n\nSocially, too, I’ve stopped going to some of my book club things and walking club activities. Not all of them, but enough that people have noticed. It’s just that getting ready feels like this whole ordeal, and then once I’m there I’m thinking about my neck instead of enjoying myself. So sometimes it feels easier to just not go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your perception of your face and neck is accurate, or could it be that you're seeing something that isn't really there?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows you’re asking whether maybe I’m seeing it wrong, and I wish I could say, “Yes, maybe it’s not really there.” But when I look in the mirror, it feels accurate to me. It feels like I can see this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and down into my neck, and once I notice it, I can’t unsee it.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in, because I can’t stop seeing it. Even if other people tell me, “No, it looks normal,” or “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” it doesn’t really settle in for me. I’ll think, well, maybe they’re just being polite, or they didn’t look closely, or they don’t want to embarrass me.\n\nAnd then when I’m around families at work, or nurses, or people at church, I feel like it’s right there, like it’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. So… I guess I can understand that maybe I’m seeing it more strongly than other people do, but it doesn’t feel imaginary to me. It feels very real when I see it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance mostly about this specific uneven spot on your face, or is it more about feeling unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one specific area, the uneven spot around my jaw and neck. I’m not saying I never worry about how I look overall, because of course once I get upset about that spot, then I start feeling ugly or embarrassed in a more general way. But the thing that starts it, the thing I came in about, is that sagging, uneven-looking part.\n\nIt’s like my eyes go straight to it every time I see myself. And then I start thinking other people are seeing the same thing. At work, with the families or the nurses, or at church, I’ll feel like when they look at me, that’s what they notice first. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” or “I don’t see anything,” I don’t really believe it. I keep thinking they’re just being nice or they don’t want to hurt my feelings.\n\nSo no, it’s not really just feeling unattractive in general. It’s very focused on that area of my face and neck.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides the uneven spot on your face and neck, that you worry other people might notice or comment on?\n\nPatient: Not really anything separate, no. I mean, I can pick at other things if I’m already upset, like my face in general or whether I look older or tired, but that’s not really the main thing I’m afraid people are noticing.\n\nIt keeps coming back to that same sagging, uneven-looking spot around my jaw and into my neck. That’s what I came in about, because I just can’t stop seeing it. Even when people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really believe them. I feel like when families at work look at me, or the nurses, or people at church, that’s the first thing their eyes go to. And then I start wondering if they’re thinking about it or if they’ll say something, even if they don’t.\n\nSo… no, it’s not like I have a whole list of features I’m worried about. It’s mostly that one area, and then everything else kind of spirals from there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the unevenness you see in your face and neck isn't actually there?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess there’s a tiny part of me that can say maybe it isn’t as obvious as it feels to me. I know people keep telling me it looks normal, and I hear that. But when I’m actually looking at myself, especially around my jaw and down into my neck, I can’t make myself believe it’s not there.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because I can’t stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot. It’s like my eyes go right to it, and then I start thinking, how could anyone else not see it too? When I’m with families at work, or around the nurses, or even people at church, I feel like it’s the first thing they notice when they look at me.\n\nSo… is there a possibility? I suppose maybe, in some logical way. But it doesn’t feel that way to me. It feels real, and when people say they don’t see it, I just think they’re being kind or trying not to embarrass me.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance something that you think would be resolved if this one uneven spot on your face and neck were fixed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, in my mind, if that one spot around my jaw and neck were fixed, I feel like I could finally stop thinking about it all the time. That’s what I keep coming back to. I came in because I can’t stop seeing that sagging, uneven-looking area, and it feels like everything else starts from there.\n\nLike, if I’m getting ready for work or church, I’m not standing there thinking about ten different things. My eyes go right to that part of my face and neck, and then I start worrying that families at work are going to notice it, or the nurses are going to see it when they’re talking to me, or people at church will look at me and that’s the first thing they’ll notice. Even when people say it looks normal, I don’t really feel reassured.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do think if that one area were fixed, I’d feel a lot better. Maybe I’d stop checking it so much or stop feeling like everyone is looking right there. I know that probably sounds like I’m putting a lot on one little area, but it doesn’t feel little to me. It feels like the whole problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be self-conscious about your appearance if the uneven spot on your face and neck were completely fixed?\n\nPatient: I think… if it were really completely fixed, I want to say no, I wouldn’t be so self-conscious. Because it really does all come back to that one sagging, uneven-looking spot around my jaw and into my neck. That’s the thing I came in about. I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nI mean, when I look in the mirror, my eyes go right there. And then once I see it, I start thinking about everybody else seeing it too. Like at work, when I’m talking to families, I feel like they’re looking right at that area. Or the nurses, when they’re standing close to me, I’ll wonder if they noticed it. Even at church, I feel like people’s eyes go there first when they look at my face.\n\nSo if that part were somehow completely fixed and looked even and normal to me, I think I’d be much less self-conscious. Maybe I’d still have normal worries, like anybody my age might, but not this constant feeling that there’s something wrong that everyone is noticing. Even when people tell me it looks normal now, I just don’t feel like I can believe them, because to me it stands out so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your appearance if everyone around you told you that you look fine and that the uneven spot on your face and neck is not noticeable?\n\nPatient: Honestly… yes, I think I would still worry. I wish I could say that if everybody around me told me it was fine, I’d just accept it and move on, but that hasn’t really been how it works for me.\n\nPeople do tell me it looks normal, or they’ll say they don’t notice anything, and I know they probably mean well. But when I look at myself, especially that sagging, uneven place around my jaw and down into my neck, it just jumps out at me. It’s like I can’t unsee it once I’ve noticed it.\n\nAnd then when I’m at work with families, or talking to the nurses, or even sitting with people at church, I still feel like that’s the first thing they’re seeing when they look at me. Even if they say they don’t, part of me thinks maybe they’re just being polite, or maybe they noticed but don’t want to make me feel bad.\n\nSo yes… even if everyone said I looked fine, I think I’d still be stuck on it, because I can’t stop seeing it myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not completely. I mean, it would probably feel nice if people thought I was attractive, of course, but that’s not really the part my mind gets stuck on. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “Do people think I’m pretty overall?” It’s more that I can’t stop seeing this one sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck.

Even if someone said, “You look good,” or “You’re attractive,” I think my brain would still go right to that area and say, “But what about this?” That’s what happens now, really. Folks will tell me I look normal, or that they don’t notice anything, and I try to take that in, but it doesn’t last. I’ll be getting ready, or I’ll catch myself in a mirror, and there it is again. It just stands out to me.

And then I start thinking about other people noticing it too, like the families I talk with at work, or the nurses when they’re close by, or people at church when they’re looking at my face. I feel like that’s the first place their eyes go, even if they say it isn’t. So being considered attractive in general… I don’t think that would fully fix it unless this specific spot looked right to me. That uneven area is really what I came in about.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess I came because I just can’t get past this thing with my face, especially around my jaw and my neck. It’s like there’s this sagging, uneven spot there, and I see it every time I look in the mirror. I know other people say it looks normal, or they act like they don’t know what I’m talking about, but to me it’s just… right there.\n\nAnd I feel like when families come in where I work, or nurses, or even people at church, that’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. Like they’re looking right at that part of my jaw before they even hear what I’m saying. I’ve tried to tell myself maybe I’m making too much of it, but I can’t stop seeing it. It’s been really hard to just go about my day without thinking everybody can see it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over to try to fix or check this uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. More than I probably want to admit. Before I leave for work, especially, I’ll think I’m ready, like I have my bag and my keys and everything, and then I’ll end up going back into the bathroom just to check one more time. I’ll look at my neck and my jaw from the front, then kind of turn to the side, and then I’ll fix my collar or pull my hair forward a certain way so it covers that area better.\n\nAnd then I’ll leave the bathroom, but sometimes I don’t even make it to the door before I turn around and go back again. I’ll check it in my phone camera too, because I think maybe the mirror is making it look different, but then the phone makes me look at it even more. If I have a meeting or I know I’m going to be around people, it gets worse.\n\nSometimes I ask my daughter, like, “Does this look strange?” or “Can you see this right here?” and I know she gets tired of me asking. She’ll say it looks fine, but I don’t really feel reassured for very long. It’s embarrassing, but yes, I keep checking and adjusting, and I’ve been late more than once because I couldn’t stop going back to look.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other habits or things you feel like you need to do over and over that aren't related to your face or appearance?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think. I mean, I’m trying to think if there’s anything else like that, like checking the stove or washing my hands a certain number of times or things people talk about, but nothing really comes to mind.\n\nI can be particular about how I get ready in the morning, but honestly that mostly circles back to how I look before I go out. I don’t feel like I have other rituals that I have to do over and over, at least not that I’ve noticed. It’s mostly this… this checking and fixing with my face and neck.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your face and neck affected your relationships or work in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I like to admit. At work, there have been chances where I could’ve helped lead a meeting or taken on more of the supervisory parts of things, and I just… I don’t volunteer. I’ll tell myself I’m too busy or that someone else would do it better, but really it’s because I picture everyone sitting there looking at my face, especially this area under my jaw and neck, instead of listening to me.\n\nAnd it affects the regular parts of my job too. Sometimes I get stuck before work trying to make myself look presentable, adjusting my hair or collar, and then I feel rushed and scattered once I get there. I’ve fallen behind on paperwork because I’m already behind from all that, or because I’m distracted thinking about how I look when people come up to talk to me.\n\nWith relationships… I still see people, but I’ve stopped going to some things I used to enjoy. I’m in a book club, and there’s a walking group too, and I’ve skipped them more than once because getting ready just feels like this whole exhausting process. By the time I’ve checked and fixed everything, I’m upset or embarrassed, and I think, “I can’t go now.” So I just stay home, even though part of me knows I probably would’ve felt better if I went.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about this uneven spot on your face when you're not intentionally trying to think about it?\n\nPatient: Yes… all the time, honestly. That’s really why I came in, because it’s not just when I’m standing in front of the mirror on purpose. It’s like my mind goes right back to that spot around my jaw and neck even when I’m trying to think about something else.\n\nI’ll be at work, talking with families or nurses, and part of me is trying to listen and do my job, but another part of me is thinking, “They’re looking at it. They can see that sagging there. They notice one side doesn’t sit right.” Even if nobody says anything, I just feel like it’s the first thing people see when they look at me.\n\nAnd at church too… I’ll be sitting there trying to pay attention, and then I’ll get this thought about whether the light is hitting my neck in a bad way, or whether someone behind me can see it from the side. People tell me I look normal, or they say they don’t know what I’m talking about, but it doesn’t really settle in. I still feel like it stands out so much. It’s just kind of always there in the back of my mind.\n\nDoctor: How would you describe the level of distress you feel when you think about this uneven spot on your face?\n\nPatient: It gets really upsetting. I mean, I know maybe that sounds vague, but it’s not just a little annoyance where I notice it and move on. When I think about that uneven, sagging area around my jaw and neck, I feel this drop in my stomach, like I’ve suddenly been caught looking awful or something.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in. I feel like I can’t stop seeing it. Even if I’m not trying to look for it, my mind goes right there, and then once I’m thinking about it, it’s hard to pull myself away from it. I’ll tell myself, “Okay, people have said it looks normal, they don’t see what you see,” but it doesn’t feel normal to me. It feels like it’s the first thing families at work notice, or nurses, or people at church when they look at me.\n\nSo the distress is… I’d say pretty strong. Not every single second is the same, but when it hits, I feel embarrassed and tense and kind of ashamed, like I want to hide that part of my face or make sure I’m turned the right way. It can take over my whole mood. Even if someone is being perfectly nice to me, I’m sitting there wondering if they’re just being polite and actually noticing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about other parts of your body besides your face and neck?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, I can have normal complaints like anyone, but the thing I can’t get away from is my face and neck, especially that sagging, uneven spot around my jaw. That’s the part I came in about, because I just can’t stop seeing it.\n\nEven when people tell me, “You look fine,” or “I don’t notice anything,” it doesn’t land for me. I still feel like it’s the first thing families at work see, or the nurses I’m around, or people at church when they look at me. So no, it’s not really my whole body. It’s very focused on that area, and it feels like it stands out so much more than anyone else seems to think it does.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your face and neck actually look uneven, or are you afraid that they might look uneven to others?\n\nPatient: I think… I think it actually looks uneven. That’s the hard part. It doesn’t feel like just a “what if” to me. When I look at it, especially around my jaw and down into my neck, I see this sagging, uneven spot, like one side just doesn’t sit the way it should.\n\nAnd then when I’m around other people, that turns into this worry that they’re seeing exactly what I’m seeing. Like at work, when I’m with families or talking with nurses, I feel like their eyes go right there, even if they’re not saying anything. Or at church, I’ll be trying to just be present, but I’m thinking, “They can notice it from this angle.”\n\nPeople have told me it looks normal, or that they don’t see what I mean, but I don’t really believe it deep down. I came in because I can’t stop seeing it, and it feels like it’s the first thing people notice when they look at my face.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern that your face and neck actually look uneven, or that others might think you look unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly that I think it actually looks uneven, that specific area. I mean, I do worry people will think I look bad because of it, but it’s not like I’m walking around thinking every part of me is unattractive in general. It’s more like my mind locks onto that sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck, and then I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nAnd once I see it, I assume other people see it too. Like families at work, or nurses I’m around, or people at church… I feel like when they look at my face, that’s the first thing they notice. Even when people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really feel reassured. I keep thinking maybe they’re just being kind or they don’t want to say it. So yes, it’s about that unevenness more than just a general fear that I’m unattractive.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face and neck ever caused you to avoid social interactions or important events because you felt too self-conscious about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has. I don’t always call it “avoiding” in my head, because I’ll make it sound more practical, like, “Oh, I’m too tired,” or “I have paperwork to catch up on,” or “It’s just not a good night.” But if I’m being honest, a lot of it comes back to not wanting people looking at my face and neck.\n\nAt work, I’ve passed up chances to help lead meetings or take on more of the supervisory-type pieces, even when I probably could do them, because the thought of standing up there or having everyone looking at me just makes me feel… exposed. I start thinking they’re going to notice that uneven sagging around my jaw, and then I get so distracted by that thought that I don’t feel like I can focus on what I’m supposed to be saying.\n\nAnd even getting ready in the morning can take so long sometimes because I’m trying to make myself look presentable, or at least less noticeable, and then I end up behind on paperwork because I’ve spent so much time checking and fixing and trying to feel okay enough to go out.\n\nSocially, too, I’ve stopped going to some of my book club things and walking club activities. Not all of them, but enough that people have noticed. It’s just that getting ready feels like this whole ordeal, and then once I’m there I’m thinking about my neck instead of enjoying myself. So sometimes it feels easier to just not go.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that your perception of your face and neck is accurate, or could it be that you're seeing something that isn't really there?\n\nPatient: I mean… part of me knows you’re asking whether maybe I’m seeing it wrong, and I wish I could say, “Yes, maybe it’s not really there.” But when I look in the mirror, it feels accurate to me. It feels like I can see this sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and down into my neck, and once I notice it, I can’t unsee it.\n\nThat’s honestly why I came in, because I can’t stop seeing it. Even if other people tell me, “No, it looks normal,” or “I don’t know what you’re talking about,” it doesn’t really settle in for me. I’ll think, well, maybe they’re just being polite, or they didn’t look closely, or they don’t want to embarrass me.\n\nAnd then when I’m around families at work, or nurses, or people at church, I feel like it’s right there, like it’s the first thing they notice when they look at me. So… I guess I can understand that maybe I’m seeing it more strongly than other people do, but it doesn’t feel imaginary to me. It feels very real when I see it.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance mostly about this specific uneven spot on your face, or is it more about feeling unattractive in general?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly that one specific area, the uneven spot around my jaw and neck. I’m not saying I never worry about how I look overall, because of course once I get upset about that spot, then I start feeling ugly or embarrassed in a more general way. But the thing that starts it, the thing I came in about, is that sagging, uneven-looking part.\n\nIt’s like my eyes go straight to it every time I see myself. And then I start thinking other people are seeing the same thing. At work, with the families or the nurses, or at church, I’ll feel like when they look at me, that’s what they notice first. Even if someone says, “You look fine,” or “I don’t see anything,” I don’t really believe it. I keep thinking they’re just being nice or they don’t want to hurt my feelings.\n\nSo no, it’s not really just feeling unattractive in general. It’s very focused on that area of my face and neck.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about your appearance, besides the uneven spot on your face and neck, that you worry other people might notice or comment on?\n\nPatient: Not really anything separate, no. I mean, I can pick at other things if I’m already upset, like my face in general or whether I look older or tired, but that’s not really the main thing I’m afraid people are noticing.\n\nIt keeps coming back to that same sagging, uneven-looking spot around my jaw and into my neck. That’s what I came in about, because I just can’t stop seeing it. Even when people tell me it looks normal, I don’t really believe them. I feel like when families at work look at me, or the nurses, or people at church, that’s the first thing their eyes go to. And then I start wondering if they’re thinking about it or if they’ll say something, even if they don’t.\n\nSo… no, it’s not like I have a whole list of features I’m worried about. It’s mostly that one area, and then everything else kind of spirals from there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's any possibility that the unevenness you see in your face and neck isn't actually there?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess there’s a tiny part of me that can say maybe it isn’t as obvious as it feels to me. I know people keep telling me it looks normal, and I hear that. But when I’m actually looking at myself, especially around my jaw and down into my neck, I can’t make myself believe it’s not there.\n\nThat’s really why I came in, because I can’t stop seeing this sagging, uneven spot. It’s like my eyes go right to it, and then I start thinking, how could anyone else not see it too? When I’m with families at work, or around the nurses, or even people at church, I feel like it’s the first thing they notice when they look at me.\n\nSo… is there a possibility? I suppose maybe, in some logical way. But it doesn’t feel that way to me. It feels real, and when people say they don’t see it, I just think they’re being kind or trying not to embarrass me.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your appearance something that you think would be resolved if this one uneven spot on your face and neck were fixed?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, in my mind, if that one spot around my jaw and neck were fixed, I feel like I could finally stop thinking about it all the time. That’s what I keep coming back to. I came in because I can’t stop seeing that sagging, uneven-looking area, and it feels like everything else starts from there.\n\nLike, if I’m getting ready for work or church, I’m not standing there thinking about ten different things. My eyes go right to that part of my face and neck, and then I start worrying that families at work are going to notice it, or the nurses are going to see it when they’re talking to me, or people at church will look at me and that’s the first thing they’ll notice. Even when people say it looks normal, I don’t really feel reassured.\n\nSo yes, I guess I do think if that one area were fixed, I’d feel a lot better. Maybe I’d stop checking it so much or stop feeling like everyone is looking right there. I know that probably sounds like I’m putting a lot on one little area, but it doesn’t feel little to me. It feels like the whole problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be self-conscious about your appearance if the uneven spot on your face and neck were completely fixed?\n\nPatient: I think… if it were really completely fixed, I want to say no, I wouldn’t be so self-conscious. Because it really does all come back to that one sagging, uneven-looking spot around my jaw and into my neck. That’s the thing I came in about. I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nI mean, when I look in the mirror, my eyes go right there. And then once I see it, I start thinking about everybody else seeing it too. Like at work, when I’m talking to families, I feel like they’re looking right at that area. Or the nurses, when they’re standing close to me, I’ll wonder if they noticed it. Even at church, I feel like people’s eyes go there first when they look at my face.\n\nSo if that part were somehow completely fixed and looked even and normal to me, I think I’d be much less self-conscious. Maybe I’d still have normal worries, like anybody my age might, but not this constant feeling that there’s something wrong that everyone is noticing. Even when people tell me it looks normal now, I just don’t feel like I can believe them, because to me it stands out so much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still worry about your appearance if everyone around you told you that you look fine and that the uneven spot on your face and neck is not noticeable?\n\nPatient: Honestly… yes, I think I would still worry. I wish I could say that if everybody around me told me it was fine, I’d just accept it and move on, but that hasn’t really been how it works for me.\n\nPeople do tell me it looks normal, or they’ll say they don’t notice anything, and I know they probably mean well. But when I look at myself, especially that sagging, uneven place around my jaw and down into my neck, it just jumps out at me. It’s like I can’t unsee it once I’ve noticed it.\n\nAnd then when I’m at work with families, or talking to the nurses, or even sitting with people at church, I still feel like that’s the first thing they’re seeing when they look at me. Even if they say they don’t, part of me thinks maybe they’re just being polite, or maybe they noticed but don’t want to make me feel bad.\n\nSo yes… even if everyone said I looked fine, I think I’d still be stuck on it, because I can’t stop seeing it myself.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about your face and neck something that you think would be completely resolved if you were considered attractive by others, regardless of this specific uneven spot?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not completely. I mean, it would probably feel nice if people thought I was attractive, of course, but that’s not really the part my mind gets stuck on. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “Do people think I’m pretty overall?” It’s more that I can’t stop seeing this one sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and neck.\n\nEven if someone said, “You look good,” or “You’re attractive,” I think my brain would still go right to that area and say, “But what about this?” That’s what happens now, really. It just stands out to me.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other people noticing it too, like the families I talk with at work, or the nurses when they’re close by, or people at church when they’re looking at my face. I feel like that’s the first place their eyes go, even if they say it isn’t. So being considered attractive in general… I don’t think that would fully fix it unless this specific spot looked right to me. That uneven area is really what I came in about.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I guess it’s possible. I don’t want to say it’s completely impossible, because I know people have told me they don’t see it, or that it looks normal. And I hear them when they say that. I’m not trying to argue with everybody.

But when I look in the mirror, I just can’t stop seeing it — that sagging, uneven spot around my jaw and down into my neck. It’s like my eyes go there before I even mean for them to. And once I see it, it feels so obvious to me that it’s hard to believe other people aren’t seeing the same thing.

So maybe it’s possible that it’s not noticeable to them the way it is to me… but it doesn’t feel that way. When I’m with families at work, or near the nurses, or around people at church, I feel like that’s the first place they look when they look at my face. Even if nobody says anything, I’m thinking, “They noticed it.” So yes, maybe it’s possible, but I have a really hard time believing it.
